The guy at the hardware store—Hank, I think his name is, wears these thick glasses—he swore by the blue one. Said the red one gums up the works on the newer models. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Making decisions. Yeah, I don't have any trouble knowing what to do or how to do it. I just... I get sidetracked before I actually get it done.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating questions or not remembering recent conversations, even after they've happened?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is actually pretty sharp. I don't forget what people tell me or ask the same thing over and over. I mean, just yesterday I was talking to my brother, Rick, and I can tell you exactly what we talked about. He just bought this pontoon boat for the lake up north. It's one of those big ones with the wrap-around seating and a built-in cooler. \n\nWe used to go out on that exact same lake when we were kids, but back then we just had this beat-up aluminum rowboat. My dad bought it at a garage sale for like fifty bucks. It leaked a little by the stern, so we always had to bring an old plastic Folgers coffee can to bail the water out. You know those big red plastic cans? They don't really make them like that anymore, now it's all weird cardboard or whatever. Anyway, Dad would sit back there with his lucky bucket hat—smelled awful, like old bait and cheap cigars—and we'd fish for hours. Never caught much, mostly just little sunfish, but it was nice getting out of the house. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question again? Oh, repeating things. Right. No, I remember conversations just fine. As long as I'm actually tuned in when it's happening, it stays up there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble planning or organizing more complex tasks, like managing your finances or following a recipe with multiple steps?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I have trouble planning things out like that. I mean, I still do all the bills and keep track of the checking account just fine. I actually have a whole system for it, with these colored folders I keep in the desk drawer. The desk is this beautiful old oak roll-top I found at an estate sale a few years back. The guy who owned it was an architect, I think. Had all these little cubby holes perfect for sorting mail. \n\nI had to refinish the whole thing myself, though. The original varnish was completely cracked and peeling. Took me three weekends just to strip it down, smelling like turpentine the whole time. My wife made me sleep in the guest room because she couldn't stand the smell. The guest room mattress is terrible, by the way. It's one of those old spring ones that pokes you right in the ribs. We really need to get a new one, maybe one of those memory foam things they sell in the boxes now. Have you ever tried one of those? I don't understand how they get a whole mattress into a box that small. It seems like it would never expand right, you know? \n\nBut, uh... wait. Organizing things. Yeah, no, I can plan out the bills and follow recipes and all that without any real trouble. The planning part is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, like your wife or close friends, noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has definitely noticed I've been struggling more with keeping on track. It's been noticeably harder for about 11 months now, ever since my cousin moved in with us after his knee surgery. He had a full replacement, you know, the titanium joint and everything. The recovery for that is brutal. He was practically living in our recliner in the living room for the first month. It’s a La-Z-Boy, one of the really plush microfiber ones. Chocolate brown. We got it on sale at this furniture outlet out by the highway. \n\nThe salesman there was a real character, kept trying to upsell us on the stain-resistant coating. I told him, look buddy, we don't have little kids running around spilling juice boxes anymore, we don't need the coating. But he just kept pushing it. Said it protects against pet hair too, which doesn't make any sense because we don't even have a dog. I always wanted a golden retriever, though. They're such loyal dogs. My neighbor growing up had one named Buster who used to fetch the morning paper... \n\nWait, what was I talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, my wife has definitely noticed it's been a lot harder for me to stay focused for the last 11 months or so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to remember important information or handle daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: Compared to a year ago? No, I don't think there's been any real change. I feel like I handle my daily stuff about the same as I did back then. Actually, a year ago was right around when I finally got around to cleaning out the gutters. I remember because I had to borrow my neighbor's extension ladder. Mine is just a little six-foot aluminum step ladder, totally useless for reaching the second story. \n\nMy neighbor, Tom, he has all the best tools. He used to be a general contractor before he retired. He's got this massive workshop in his garage with a table saw, a drill press, the works. He even built his own kitchen cabinets from scratch out of cherry wood. Beautiful grain on those. He put on these brushed brass handles that really make the wood pop. I was thinking of redoing our kitchen hardware too, maybe going with a matte black, but the hinges are so old I'd probably have to replace the whole door. And once you start replacing doors, you might as well just do the countertops, you know? It never ends with houses. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what did you ask? Oh, changes from last year. Right. No, no changes really. My memory and getting my daily chores done is pretty much exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? But like I was saying, no, not really. I feel like I'm managing my daily stuff about the same as I was last year. My routine hasn't really changed at all. \n\nWell, except I did start doing the grocery shopping on Tuesday mornings instead of Thursdays. Tuesday is the senior discount day at the supermarket down the street. You get five percent off the whole cart. It doesn't sound like much, but it really adds up, especially with the price of groceries lately. Have you seen how much a carton of eggs costs now? It's absolute highway robbery. I remember when I was a kid, my grandfather kept his own chickens in the backyard so we never had to buy eggs. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were. Mean birds, though. One of them pecked me right on the shin when I was trying to help him feed them. I actually still have a tiny little white scar right there... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. No decline. My memory and how I handle things is exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any changes in your ability to learn new information, like figuring out how to use a new gadget or remembering names that you didn’t used to struggle with?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I don't think that's changed. I mean, gadgets are always a pain to figure out, but no more than usual. My son actually got me one of those smart thermostats for Christmas. You know, the round ones that light up when you walk by? It's supposed to save money on the heating bill by learning your schedule. But you have to set the whole thing up on your phone. I spent an hour just trying to get the Wi-Fi password typed in because the buttons on the screen are so tiny. My thumbs are just too big for those little digital keyboards. \n\nI really miss my old Blackberry. I had one back in the day with the actual physical buttons. That was a great phone. You could type a whole email without even looking down at the screen once you got used to it. I miss that tactile click, you know? Everything is just flat glass now. And you drop it once and it shatters into a million pieces. My nephew dropped his tablet on his driveway last month and the screen looked like a spiderweb. Cost him two hundred bucks to get the glass replaced at one of those little kiosks in the middle of the mall. The kid working there was practically a teenager himself, probably couldn't even buy a lottery ticket yet, fixing these expensive computers with a tiny screwdriver... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Gadgets, right. No, honestly, it takes me the exact same amount of time to figure that stuff out as it did last year. No real difference there.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you mentioned that you've had trouble remembering things or managing daily tasks in ways that you might not be noticing yourself?\n\nPatient: My wife? No, she hasn't said anything like that. I mean, she gets on my case about leaving my coffee mug on the coffee table instead of putting it in the dishwasher, but she's always done that. She likes things to be perfectly tidy. You should see her closet. Everything is color-coded. All the blues together, then the greens, then the reds. She even has her shoes lined up by heel height. It's impressive, honestly. \n\nI tried organizing my tool bench like that once. I bought one of those pegboards and all the little hooks. I spent a whole Saturday outlining where the hammers go, where the wrenches go... I even used a permanent marker to draw little silhouettes for each tool. It looked great for about a week. But then I was fixing the lawnmower, and I just sort of left everything out on the bench. The lawnmower was a whole ordeal, by the way. The spark plug was completely fouled. I had to go to three different hardware stores just to find the right replacement because it's an older model. The guy at the last store, he was wearing this ridiculous hat, like a fishing bucket hat indoors...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my wife. Yeah, no, she hasn't mentioned me forgetting things or slipping up with my daily stuff any more than usual. Just the normal bickering about chores, you know?\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever pointed out that you forgot something important or had trouble managing a task that you didn’t realize you were struggling with?\n\nPatient: Didn't we just talk about her? But yeah, like I said, no. Not anything important, anyway. \n\nI mean, she got a little annoyed a couple weeks ago because I forgot to pick up the dry cleaning on my way back from the hardware store. But that wasn't a big deal, it was just her blue dress and a couple of my button-downs. She wanted to wear that dress to her sister's anniversary dinner. Her sister, Susan, she married this guy who owns a string of car washes out in the suburbs. You know the ones with the giant blue spinning brushes? I always worry those things are going to scratch the clear coat on my truck. I have a '98 Ford, still runs like an absolute dream. It only has about eighty thousand miles on it because I hardly drive it anymore. The secret is changing the oil every three thousand miles, religiously. People say you can wait until five or even ten thousand miles now with that new synthetic stuff, but I just don't trust it. My dad was a mechanic and he always said oil is the lifeblood of an engine, you let it get dirty and you're just asking for a blown gasket... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did I forget something? Oh, right, my wife pointing things out. No, she hasn't said I'm struggling or forgetting important stuff. Just normal everyday slips like the dry cleaning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you used to be, say five years ago, have you noticed any real difference in your ability to remember things or stay on top of your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, five years ago? I mean, yeah, I guess there's been a change. Lately, I've been forgetting things I actually meant to do. Like, I was supposed to return a call from my doctor's office about some blood work, and it just completely slipped my mind. And picking up my prescription, too. I went all the way to the pharmacy, but then I realized I hadn't even checked if it was ready, and it wasn't. The pharmacist there, he's a nice guy, always has a bowl of those little strawberry candies on the counter. You know the ones with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? My grandmother used to keep those in her purse. She had this huge leather purse, weighed a ton. She used to carry around a roll of quarters in it, just in case she needed to make a phone call or pay for parking. Parking meters used to take quarters, not these fancy credit card apps they have now. You have to download an app just to park downtown! It’s ridiculous. I parked down by the courthouse last month and stood there for ten minutes trying to scan a barcode on the meter... \n\nAnyway, I also ended up leaving the house late the other day when I was supposed to take my cousin to one of his appointments. Just couldn't get out the door on time. So yeah, I guess I am a little more forgetful with my daily stuff than I used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My wife? Well, we just talked about her, didn't we? But no, she hasn't said anything about it getting worse over the last year. I mean, she hasn't said, "Hey, you're more forgetful now." We've been married for thirty-two years, so she's pretty used to how I am. We actually met at a roller rink. Can you believe that? They used to have those couples' skates where they'd play a slow song, usually something by Chicago or maybe REO Speedwagon. I wasn't a very good skater, honestly. I kept hugging the wall, but she was out there doing crossovers and spinning. She wore these bright yellow leg warmers. Everybody wore leg warmers back then, even if they weren't going to the gym. It was just the style. Like those shoulder pads in the blazers? My sister had a blazer with shoulder pads so big she looked like a linebacker for the Bears. She loved the Bears. We all did. The '85 season, man, that was something else. Walter Payton... 

But, uh, what was the question? Has she noticed it getting worse? No, I don't think she's noticed any big change in the last year or so. Just the usual stuff we talked about.

I don't understand how they get a whole mattress into a box that small. It seems like it would never expand right, you know? \n\nBut, uh... wait. Organizing things. Yeah, no, I can plan out the bills and follow recipes and all that without any real trouble. The planning part is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, like your wife or close friends, noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has definitely noticed I've been struggling more with keeping on track. It's been noticeably harder for about 11 months now, ever since my cousin moved in with us after his knee surgery. He had a full replacement, you know, the titanium joint and everything. The recovery for that is brutal. He was practically living in our recliner in the living room for the first month. It’s a La-Z-Boy, one of the really plush microfiber ones. Chocolate brown. We got it on sale at this furniture outlet out by the highway. \n\nThe salesman there was a real character, kept trying to upsell us on the stain-resistant coating. I told him, look buddy, we don't have little kids running around spilling juice boxes anymore, we don't need the coating. But he just kept pushing it. Said it protects against pet hair too, which doesn't make any sense because we don't even have a dog. I always wanted a golden retriever, though. They're such loyal dogs. My neighbor growing up had one named Buster who used to fetch the morning paper... \n\nWait, what was I talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, my wife has definitely noticed it's been a lot harder for me to stay focused for the last 11 months or so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to remember important information or handle daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: Compared to a year ago? No, I don't think there's been any real change. I feel like I handle my daily stuff about the same as I did back then. Actually, a year ago was right around when I finally got around to cleaning out the gutters. I remember because I had to borrow my neighbor's extension ladder. Mine is just a little six-foot aluminum step ladder, totally useless for reaching the second story. \n\nMy neighbor, Tom, he has all the best tools. He used to be a general contractor before he retired. He's got this massive workshop in his garage with a table saw, a drill press, the works. He even built his own kitchen cabinets from scratch out of cherry wood. Beautiful grain on those. He put on these brushed brass handles that really make the wood pop. I was thinking of redoing our kitchen hardware too, maybe going with a matte black, but the hinges are so old I'd probably have to replace the whole door. And once you start replacing doors, you might as well just do the countertops, you know? It never ends with houses. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what did you ask? Oh, changes from last year. Right. No, no changes really. My memory and getting my daily chores done is pretty much exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? But like I was saying, no, not really. I feel like I'm managing my daily stuff about the same as I was last year. My routine hasn't really changed at all. \n\nWell, except I did start doing the grocery shopping on Tuesday mornings instead of Thursdays. Tuesday is the senior discount day at the supermarket down the street. You get five percent off the whole cart. It doesn't sound like much, but it really adds up, especially with the price of groceries lately. Have you seen how much a carton of eggs costs now? It's absolute highway robbery. I remember when I was a kid, my grandfather kept his own chickens in the backyard so we never had to buy eggs. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were. Mean birds, though. One of them pecked me right on the shin when I was trying to help him feed them. I actually still have a tiny little white scar right there... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. No decline. My memory and how I handle things is exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any changes in your ability to learn new information, like figuring out how to use a new gadget or remembering names that you didn’t used to struggle with?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I don't think that's changed. I mean, gadgets are always a pain to figure out, but no more than usual. My son actually got me one of those smart thermostats for Christmas. You know, the round ones that light up when you walk by? It's supposed to save money on the heating bill by learning your schedule. But you have to set the whole thing up on your phone. I spent an hour just trying to get the Wi-Fi password typed in because the buttons on the screen are so tiny. My thumbs are just too big for those little digital keyboards. \n\nI really miss my old Blackberry. I had one back in the day with the actual physical buttons. That was a great phone. You could type a whole email without even looking down at the screen once you got used to it. I miss that tactile click, you know? Everything is just flat glass now. And you drop it once and it shatters into a million pieces. My nephew dropped his tablet on his driveway last month and the screen looked like a spiderweb. Cost him two hundred bucks to get the glass replaced at one of those little kiosks in the middle of the mall. The kid working there was practically a teenager himself, probably couldn't even buy a lottery ticket yet, fixing these expensive computers with a tiny screwdriver... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Gadgets, right. No, honestly, it takes me the exact same amount of time to figure that stuff out as it did last year. No real difference there.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you mentioned that you've had trouble remembering things or managing daily tasks in ways that you might not be noticing yourself?\n\nPatient: My wife? No, she hasn't said anything like that. I mean, she gets on my case about leaving my coffee mug on the coffee table instead of putting it in the dishwasher, but she's always done that. She likes things to be perfectly tidy. You should see her closet. Everything is color-coded. All the blues together, then the greens, then the reds. She even has her shoes lined up by heel height. It's impressive, honestly. \n\nI tried organizing my tool bench like that once. I bought one of those pegboards and all the little hooks. I spent a whole Saturday outlining where the hammers go, where the wrenches go... I even used a permanent marker to draw little silhouettes for each tool. It looked great for about a week. But then I was fixing the lawnmower, and I just sort of left everything out on the bench. The lawnmower was a whole ordeal, by the way. The spark plug was completely fouled. I had to go to three different hardware stores just to find the right replacement because it's an older model. The guy at the last store, he was wearing this ridiculous hat, like a fishing bucket hat indoors...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my wife. Yeah, no, she hasn't mentioned me forgetting things or slipping up with my daily stuff any more than usual. Just the normal bickering about chores, you know?\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever pointed out that you forgot something important or had trouble managing a task that you didn’t realize you were struggling with?\n\nPatient: Didn't we just talk about her? But yeah, like I said, no. Not anything important, anyway. \n\nI mean, she got a little annoyed a couple weeks ago because I forgot to pick up the dry cleaning on my way back from the hardware store. But that wasn't a big deal, it was just her blue dress and a couple of my button-downs. She wanted to wear that dress to her sister's anniversary dinner. Her sister, Susan, she married this guy who owns a string of car washes out in the suburbs. You know the ones with the giant blue spinning brushes? I always worry those things are going to scratch the clear coat on my truck. I have a '98 Ford, still runs like an absolute dream. It only has about eighty thousand miles on it because I hardly drive it anymore. The secret is changing the oil every three thousand miles, religiously. People say you can wait until five or even ten thousand miles now with that new synthetic stuff, but I just don't trust it. My dad was a mechanic and he always said oil is the lifeblood of an engine, you let it get dirty and you're just asking for a blown gasket... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did I forget something? Oh, right, my wife pointing things out. No, she hasn't said I'm struggling or forgetting important stuff. Just normal everyday slips like the dry cleaning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you used to be, say five years ago, have you noticed any real difference in your ability to remember things or stay on top of your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, five years ago? I mean, yeah, I guess there's been a change. Lately, I've been forgetting things I actually meant to do. Like, I was supposed to return a call from my doctor's office about some blood work, and it just completely slipped my mind. And picking up my prescription, too. I went all the way to the pharmacy, but then I realized I hadn't even checked if it was ready, and it wasn't. The pharmacist there, he's a nice guy, always has a bowl of those little strawberry candies on the counter. You know the ones with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? My grandmother used to keep those in her purse. She had this huge leather purse, weighed a ton. She used to carry around a roll of quarters in it, just in case she needed to make a phone call or pay for parking. Parking meters used to take quarters, not these fancy credit card apps they have now. You have to download an app just to park downtown! It’s ridiculous. I parked down by the courthouse last month and stood there for ten minutes trying to scan a barcode on the meter... \n\nAnyway, I also ended up leaving the house late the other day when I was supposed to take my cousin to one of his appointments. Just couldn't get out the door on time. So yeah, I guess I am a little more forgetful with my daily stuff than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: My wife? Well, we just talked about her, didn't we? But no, she hasn't said anything about it getting worse over the last year. I mean, she hasn't said, \"Hey, you're more forgetful now.\" We've been married for thirty-two years, so she's pretty used to how I am. We actually met at a roller rink. Can you believe that? They used to have those couples' skates where they'd play a slow song, usually something by Chicago or maybe REO Speedwagon. I wasn't a very good skater, honestly. I kept hugging the wall, but she was out there doing crossovers and spinning. She wore these bright yellow leg warmers. Everybody wore leg warmers back then, even if they weren't going to the gym. It was just the style. Like those shoulder pads in the blazers? My sister had a blazer with shoulder pads so big she looked like a linebacker for the Bears. She loved the Bears. We all did. The '85 season, man, that was something else. Walter Payton... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Has she noticed it getting worse? Well, a year ago? Let me think... I guess it has been noticeably harder for about, oh, eleven months now. Yeah, right around then. That's when my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. He had his knee replaced. The right one, I think. Or maybe the left? No, it was the right, because he couldn't drive his truck for a while, and it's an automatic. My uncle had this old Chevy pickup, farm truck, really. We used to haul hay in the back of it during the summer. Baling hay is tough work, let me tell you. The dust gets everywhere, up your nose, in your eyes, and those bales are heavy. Especially if they get a little wet before you get them in the barn. We had this huge red barn, classic style, with a tin roof that sounded amazing when it rained... 

Wait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking about a year ago, right? Yeah, so like I said, about eleven months ago, ever since my cousin came to stay with us, things have definitely been harder to manage. Just a lot more to keep track of, I suppose.

Does this happen in other situations too, like remembering appointments or where you put things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it does happen with other stuff. Lately I’ve been forgetting things I really meant to do. Like just last week, I was supposed to return a call from the doctor’s office. The receptionist there, Brenda... she actually used to work down at the old hardware store on Main Street. You know, the one that burned down back in '04? Or maybe it was '05. Terrible fire. They said it was faulty wiring, but they had this ancient space heater behind the counter that always looked like a real hazard to me, just sitting right next to all those cardboard boxes. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I completely forgot to call them back. And picking up my prescriptions, that slips my mind a lot too. Or like, leaving the house on time for one of my cousin’s appointments. I’m always running late for those now. He goes to this clinic over in Westlake, and the parking there is just an absolute nightmare. You have to park three blocks away and walk, and with his bad knee... well, I keep telling him we should just take a cab, but he's way too stubborn to spend the money. But yeah, I just... I forget to look at the clock and get out the door when I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about the date, where you are, or have trouble recognizing people you know well?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I know what day it is, and I know my own house, and my wife, and all that. I'm not... my mind's not going in that way, you know? Like my Uncle Earl, now he got really bad toward the end. He used to wander out of his house in the middle of the night in his pajamas, looking for his dog. But the dog had been dead for ten years. It was a Golden Retriever named Buster. Smartest dog you ever met, too. You could throw a tennis ball into the lake, and Buster would swim out and get it, even if the water was freezing cold. Earl loved that dog more than anything. But no, I don't get confused like that. I just... I just don't pay attention, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're having a conversation or trying to follow along with something, like a TV show or instructions, do you find yourself getting lost or confused, even if people explain it more than once?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that. I don't get confused by what people are saying, and I can follow a TV show just fine. Well, mostly. I mean, my wife loves those British murder mysteries, the ones where they all live in those huge manor houses with the big gardens. We watched one last night where the gardener did it. Or wait, no, it was the butler. It's always the butler, right? But the gardens in those shows... they're incredible. I tried to grow prize-winning tomatoes once, back when we had the house on Elm Street. Had this whole setup with special soil and everything, but the squirrels just absolutely ruined it. Those little menaces would take one bite out of a perfectly good tomato and just leave it there on the vine. It was infuriating. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, following along. Yeah, if I'm actually listening, I understand it just fine. It's just that half the time my brain is off thinking about squirrels or something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or focus on tasks, like reading or following a conversation, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. It's really frustrating, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order for the church, or I'm listening through one of those long parish planning calls... I start off fine. I'm right there, pen in hand. But before long, my mind just completely wanders off. I'll realize I haven't heard a word anyone said for the last five minutes, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never even read them. \n\nWhich reminds me, the catering for the church picnic last year was a disaster. We ordered from this new deli that opened up where the old bakery used to be. You know, the bakery that had those amazing bear claws? My wife and I used to go there every Sunday after service. The owner, Sal, he was such a character. Always had a joke ready. He retired to Florida, I think. Boca Raton, maybe? Or Naples. Somewhere warm. Anyway, this new deli, they completely messed up the potato salad. Put way too much mustard in it. People were complaining all afternoon. But yeah, trying to focus on those planning calls... it's like my brain just packs up and leaves the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble making decisions, solving problems, or figuring out the steps to complete tasks you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can still figure things out just fine, make decisions, all that. Like, if I need to fix something around the house or plan out my week, I know how to do it. It's just getting myself to actually finish it that's the problem. \n\nSpeaking of fixing things, I finally got around to looking at the lawnmower last weekend. It's one of those riding mowers, a John Deere. Green and yellow, you know the ones. I bought it second-hand from my neighbor, Gary, when he moved to a condo downtown. Gary's a good guy, but he never really took care of his tools. I had to replace the spark plugs and clean out the carburetor. It's amazing how much gunk builds up in there if you don't use the right fuel stabilizer over the winter. I always use the blue stuff, not the red stuff. The guy at the hardware store—Hank, I think his name is, wears these thick glasses—he swore by the blue one. Said the red one gums up the works on the newer models. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Making decisions. Yeah, I don't have any trouble knowing what to do or how to do it. I just... I get sidetracked before I actually get it done.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating questions or not remembering recent conversations, even after they've happened?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is actually pretty sharp. I don't forget what people tell me or ask the same thing over and over. I mean, just yesterday I was talking to my brother, Rick, and I can tell you exactly what we talked about. He just bought this pontoon boat for the lake up north. It's one of those big ones with the wrap-around seating and a built-in cooler. \n\nWe used to go out on that exact same lake when we were kids, but back then we just had this beat-up aluminum rowboat. My dad bought it at a garage sale for like fifty bucks. It leaked a little by the stern, so we always had to bring an old plastic Folgers coffee can to bail the water out. You know those big red plastic cans? They don't really make them like that anymore, now it's all weird cardboard or whatever. Anyway, Dad would sit back there with his lucky bucket hat—smelled awful, like old bait and cheap cigars—and we'd fish for hours. Never caught much, mostly just little sunfish, but it was nice getting out of the house. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question again? Oh, repeating things. Right. No, I remember conversations just fine. As long as I'm actually tuned in when it's happening, it stays up there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble planning or organizing more complex tasks, like managing your finances or following a recipe with multiple steps?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I have trouble planning things out like that. I mean, I still do all the bills and keep track of the checking account just fine. I actually have a whole system for it, with these colored folders I keep in the desk drawer. The desk is this beautiful old oak roll-top I found at an estate sale a few years back. The guy who owned it was an architect, I think. Had all these little cubby holes perfect for sorting mail. \n\nI had to refinish the whole thing myself, though. The original varnish was completely cracked and peeling. Took me three weekends just to strip it down, smelling like turpentine the whole time. My wife made me sleep in the guest room because she couldn't stand the smell. The guest room mattress is terrible, by the way. It's one of those old spring ones that pokes you right in the ribs. We really need to get a new one, maybe one of those memory foam things they sell in the boxes now. Have you ever tried one of those? I don't understand how they get a whole mattress into a box that small. It seems like it would never expand right, you know? \n\nBut, uh... wait. Organizing things. Yeah, no, I can plan out the bills and follow recipes and all that without any real trouble. The planning part is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, like your wife or close friends, noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has definitely noticed I've been struggling more with keeping on track. It's been noticeably harder for about 11 months now, ever since my cousin moved in with us after his knee surgery. He had a full replacement, you know, the titanium joint and everything. The recovery for that is brutal. He was practically living in our recliner in the living room for the first month. It’s a La-Z-Boy, one of the really plush microfiber ones. Chocolate brown. We got it on sale at this furniture outlet out by the highway. \n\nThe salesman there was a real character, kept trying to upsell us on the stain-resistant coating. I told him, look buddy, we don't have little kids running around spilling juice boxes anymore, we don't need the coating. But he just kept pushing it. Said it protects against pet hair too, which doesn't make any sense because we don't even have a dog. I always wanted a golden retriever, though. They're such loyal dogs. My neighbor growing up had one named Buster who used to fetch the morning paper... \n\nWait, what was I talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, my wife has definitely noticed it's been a lot harder for me to stay focused for the last 11 months or so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to remember important information or handle daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: Compared to a year ago? No, I don't think there's been any real change. I feel like I handle my daily stuff about the same as I did back then. Actually, a year ago was right around when I finally got around to cleaning out the gutters. I remember because I had to borrow my neighbor's extension ladder. Mine is just a little six-foot aluminum step ladder, totally useless for reaching the second story. \n\nMy neighbor, Tom, he has all the best tools. He used to be a general contractor before he retired. He's got this massive workshop in his garage with a table saw, a drill press, the works. He even built his own kitchen cabinets from scratch out of cherry wood. Beautiful grain on those. He put on these brushed brass handles that really make the wood pop. I was thinking of redoing our kitchen hardware too, maybe going with a matte black, but the hinges are so old I'd probably have to replace the whole door. And once you start replacing doors, you might as well just do the countertops, you know? It never ends with houses. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what did you ask? Oh, changes from last year. Right. No, no changes really. My memory and getting my daily chores done is pretty much exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? But like I was saying, no, not really. I feel like I'm managing my daily stuff about the same as I was last year. My routine hasn't really changed at all. \n\nWell, except I did start doing the grocery shopping on Tuesday mornings instead of Thursdays. Tuesday is the senior discount day at the supermarket down the street. You get five percent off the whole cart. It doesn't sound like much, but it really adds up, especially with the price of groceries lately. Have you seen how much a carton of eggs costs now? It's absolute highway robbery. I remember when I was a kid, my grandfather kept his own chickens in the backyard so we never had to buy eggs. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were. Mean birds, though. One of them pecked me right on the shin when I was trying to help him feed them. I actually still have a tiny little white scar right there... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. No decline. My memory and how I handle things is exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any changes in your ability to learn new information, like figuring out how to use a new gadget or remembering names that you didn’t used to struggle with?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I don't think that's changed. I mean, gadgets are always a pain to figure out, but no more than usual. My son actually got me one of those smart thermostats for Christmas. You know, the round ones that light up when you walk by? It's supposed to save money on the heating bill by learning your schedule. But you have to set the whole thing up on your phone. I spent an hour just trying to get the Wi-Fi password typed in because the buttons on the screen are so tiny. My thumbs are just too big for those little digital keyboards. \n\nI really miss my old Blackberry. I had one back in the day with the actual physical buttons. That was a great phone. You could type a whole email without even looking down at the screen once you got used to it. I miss that tactile click, you know? Everything is just flat glass now. And you drop it once and it shatters into a million pieces. My nephew dropped his tablet on his driveway last month and the screen looked like a spiderweb. Cost him two hundred bucks to get the glass replaced at one of those little kiosks in the middle of the mall. The kid working there was practically a teenager himself, probably couldn't even buy a lottery ticket yet, fixing these expensive computers with a tiny screwdriver... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Gadgets, right. No, honestly, it takes me the exact same amount of time to figure that stuff out as it did last year. No real difference there.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you mentioned that you've had trouble remembering things or managing daily tasks in ways that you might not be noticing yourself?\n\nPatient: My wife? No, she hasn't said anything like that. I mean, she gets on my case about leaving my coffee mug on the coffee table instead of putting it in the dishwasher, but she's always done that. She likes things to be perfectly tidy. You should see her closet. Everything is color-coded. All the blues together, then the greens, then the reds. She even has her shoes lined up by heel height. It's impressive, honestly. \n\nI tried organizing my tool bench like that once. I bought one of those pegboards and all the little hooks. I spent a whole Saturday outlining where the hammers go, where the wrenches go... I even used a permanent marker to draw little silhouettes for each tool. It looked great for about a week. But then I was fixing the lawnmower, and I just sort of left everything out on the bench. The lawnmower was a whole ordeal, by the way. The spark plug was completely fouled. I had to go to three different hardware stores just to find the right replacement because it's an older model. The guy at the last store, he was wearing this ridiculous hat, like a fishing bucket hat indoors...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my wife. Yeah, no, she hasn't mentioned me forgetting things or slipping up with my daily stuff any more than usual. Just the normal bickering about chores, you know?\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever pointed out that you forgot something important or had trouble managing a task that you didn’t realize you were struggling with?\n\nPatient: Didn't we just talk about her? But yeah, like I said, no. Not anything important, anyway. \n\nI mean, she got a little annoyed a couple weeks ago because I forgot to pick up the dry cleaning on my way back from the hardware store. But that wasn't a big deal, it was just her blue dress and a couple of my button-downs. She wanted to wear that dress to her sister's anniversary dinner. Her sister, Susan, she married this guy who owns a string of car washes out in the suburbs. You know the ones with the giant blue spinning brushes? I always worry those things are going to scratch the clear coat on my truck. I have a '98 Ford, still runs like an absolute dream. It only has about eighty thousand miles on it because I hardly drive it anymore. The secret is changing the oil every three thousand miles, religiously. People say you can wait until five or even ten thousand miles now with that new synthetic stuff, but I just don't trust it. My dad was a mechanic and he always said oil is the lifeblood of an engine, you let it get dirty and you're just asking for a blown gasket... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did I forget something? Oh, right, my wife pointing things out. No, she hasn't said I'm struggling or forgetting important stuff. Just normal everyday slips like the dry cleaning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you used to be, say five years ago, have you noticed any real difference in your ability to remember things or stay on top of your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, five years ago? I mean, yeah, I guess there's been a change. Lately, I've been forgetting things I actually meant to do. Like, I was supposed to return a call from my doctor's office about some blood work, and it just completely slipped my mind. And picking up my prescription, too. I went all the way to the pharmacy, but then I realized I hadn't even checked if it was ready, and it wasn't. The pharmacist there, he's a nice guy, always has a bowl of those little strawberry candies on the counter. You know the ones with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? My grandmother used to keep those in her purse. She had this huge leather purse, weighed a ton. She used to carry around a roll of quarters in it, just in case she needed to make a phone call or pay for parking. Parking meters used to take quarters, not these fancy credit card apps they have now. You have to download an app just to park downtown! It’s ridiculous. I parked down by the courthouse last month and stood there for ten minutes trying to scan a barcode on the meter... \n\nAnyway, I also ended up leaving the house late the other day when I was supposed to take my cousin to one of his appointments. Just couldn't get out the door on time. So yeah, I guess I am a little more forgetful with my daily stuff than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: My wife? Well, we just talked about her, didn't we? But no, she hasn't said anything about it getting worse over the last year. I mean, she hasn't said, \"Hey, you're more forgetful now.\" We've been married for thirty-two years, so she's pretty used to how I am. We actually met at a roller rink. Can you believe that? They used to have those couples' skates where they'd play a slow song, usually something by Chicago or maybe REO Speedwagon. I wasn't a very good skater, honestly. I kept hugging the wall, but she was out there doing crossovers and spinning. She wore these bright yellow leg warmers. Everybody wore leg warmers back then, even if they weren't going to the gym. It was just the style. Like those shoulder pads in the blazers? My sister had a blazer with shoulder pads so big she looked like a linebacker for the Bears. She loved the Bears. We all did. The '85 season, man, that was something else. Walter Payton... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Has she noticed it getting worse? No, I don't think she's noticed any big change in the last year or so. Just the usual stuff we talked about.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any real change in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks effectively?\n\nPatient: Well, a year ago? Let me think... I guess it has been noticeably harder for about, oh, eleven months now. Yeah, right around then. That's when my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. He had his knee replaced. The right one, I think. Or maybe the left? No, it was the right, because he couldn't drive his truck for a while, and it's an automatic. If it was a manual, he really would have been out of luck with the clutch. You don't see many manual transmissions anymore, do you? I learned to drive on a three-on-the-tree. My uncle had this old Chevy pickup, farm truck, really. We used to haul hay in the back of it during the summer. Baling hay is tough work, let me tell you. The dust gets everywhere, up your nose, in your eyes, and those bales are heavy. Especially if they get a little wet before you get them in the barn. We had this huge red barn, classic style, with a tin roof that sounded amazing when it rained... \n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking about a year ago, right? Yeah, so like I said, about eleven months ago, ever since my cousin came to stay with us, things have definitely been harder to manage. Just a lot more to keep track of, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, no, I can plan out the bills and follow recipes and all that without any real trouble. The planning part is fine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, like your wife or close friends, noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over time?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife has definitely noticed I've been struggling more with keeping on track. It's been noticeably harder for about 11 months now, ever since my cousin moved in with us after his knee surgery. He had a full replacement, you know, the titanium joint and everything. The recovery for that is brutal. He was practically living in our recliner in the living room for the first month. It’s a La-Z-Boy, one of the really plush microfiber ones. Chocolate brown. We got it on sale at this furniture outlet out by the highway. \n\nThe salesman there was a real character, kept trying to upsell us on the stain-resistant coating. I told him, look buddy, we don't have little kids running around spilling juice boxes anymore, we don't need the coating. But he just kept pushing it. Said it protects against pet hair too, which doesn't make any sense because we don't even have a dog. I always wanted a golden retriever, though. They're such loyal dogs. My neighbor growing up had one named Buster who used to fetch the morning paper... \n\nWait, what was I talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, my wife has definitely noticed it's been a lot harder for me to stay focused for the last 11 months or so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to remember important information or handle daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: Compared to a year ago? No, I don't think there's been any real change. I feel like I handle my daily stuff about the same as I did back then. Actually, a year ago was right around when I finally got around to cleaning out the gutters. I remember because I had to borrow my neighbor's extension ladder. Mine is just a little six-foot aluminum step ladder, totally useless for reaching the second story. \n\nMy neighbor, Tom, he has all the best tools. He used to be a general contractor before he retired. He's got this massive workshop in his garage with a table saw, a drill press, the works. He even built his own kitchen cabinets from scratch out of cherry wood. Beautiful grain on those. He put on these brushed brass handles that really make the wood pop. I was thinking of redoing our kitchen hardware too, maybe going with a matte black, but the hinges are so old I'd probably have to replace the whole door. And once you start replacing doors, you might as well just do the countertops, you know? It never ends with houses. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what did you ask? Oh, changes from last year. Right. No, no changes really. My memory and getting my daily chores done is pretty much exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? But like I was saying, no, not really. I feel like I'm managing my daily stuff about the same as I was last year. My routine hasn't really changed at all. \n\nWell, except I did start doing the grocery shopping on Tuesday mornings instead of Thursdays. Tuesday is the senior discount day at the supermarket down the street. You get five percent off the whole cart. It doesn't sound like much, but it really adds up, especially with the price of groceries lately. Have you seen how much a carton of eggs costs now? It's absolute highway robbery. I remember when I was a kid, my grandfather kept his own chickens in the backyard so we never had to buy eggs. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were. Mean birds, though. One of them pecked me right on the shin when I was trying to help him feed them. I actually still have a tiny little white scar right there... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. No decline. My memory and how I handle things is exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any changes in your ability to learn new information, like figuring out how to use a new gadget or remembering names that you didn’t used to struggle with?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I don't think that's changed. I mean, gadgets are always a pain to figure out, but no more than usual. My son actually got me one of those smart thermostats for Christmas. You know, the round ones that light up when you walk by? It's supposed to save money on the heating bill by learning your schedule. But you have to set the whole thing up on your phone. I spent an hour just trying to get the Wi-Fi password typed in because the buttons on the screen are so tiny. My thumbs are just too big for those little digital keyboards. \n\nI really miss my old Blackberry. I had one back in the day with the actual physical buttons. That was a great phone. You could type a whole email without even looking down at the screen once you got used to it. I miss that tactile click, you know? Everything is just flat glass now. And you drop it once and it shatters into a million pieces. My nephew dropped his tablet on his driveway last month and the screen looked like a spiderweb. Cost him two hundred bucks to get the glass replaced at one of those little kiosks in the middle of the mall. The kid working there was practically a teenager himself, probably couldn't even buy a lottery ticket yet, fixing these expensive computers with a tiny screwdriver... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Gadgets, right. No, honestly, it takes me the exact same amount of time to figure that stuff out as it did last year. No real difference there.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you mentioned that you've had trouble remembering things or managing daily tasks in ways that you might not be noticing yourself?\n\nPatient: My wife? No, she hasn't said anything like that. I mean, she gets on my case about leaving my coffee mug on the coffee table instead of putting it in the dishwasher, but she's always done that. She likes things to be perfectly tidy. You should see her closet. Everything is color-coded. All the blues together, then the greens, then the reds. She even has her shoes lined up by heel height. It's impressive, honestly. \n\nI tried organizing my tool bench like that once. I bought one of those pegboards and all the little hooks. I spent a whole Saturday outlining where the hammers go, where the wrenches go... I even used a permanent marker to draw little silhouettes for each tool. It looked great for about a week. But then I was fixing the lawnmower, and I just sort of left everything out on the bench. The lawnmower was a whole ordeal, by the way. The spark plug was completely fouled. I had to go to three different hardware stores just to find the right replacement because it's an older model. The guy at the last store, he was wearing this ridiculous hat, like a fishing bucket hat indoors...\n\nOh, but you were asking about my wife. Yeah, no, she hasn't mentioned me forgetting things or slipping up with my daily stuff any more than usual. Just the normal bickering about chores, you know?\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever pointed out that you forgot something important or had trouble managing a task that you didn’t realize you were struggling with?\n\nPatient: Didn't we just talk about her? But yeah, like I said, no. Not anything important, anyway. \n\nI mean, she got a little annoyed a couple weeks ago because I forgot to pick up the dry cleaning on my way back from the hardware store. But that wasn't a big deal, it was just her blue dress and a couple of my button-downs. She wanted to wear that dress to her sister's anniversary dinner. Her sister, Susan, she married this guy who owns a string of car washes out in the suburbs. You know the ones with the giant blue spinning brushes? I always worry those things are going to scratch the clear coat on my truck. I have a '98 Ford, still runs like an absolute dream. It only has about eighty thousand miles on it because I hardly drive it anymore. The secret is changing the oil every three thousand miles, religiously. People say you can wait until five or even ten thousand miles now with that new synthetic stuff, but I just don't trust it. My dad was a mechanic and he always said oil is the lifeblood of an engine, you let it get dirty and you're just asking for a blown gasket... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did I forget something? Oh, right, my wife pointing things out. No, she hasn't said I'm struggling or forgetting important stuff. Just normal everyday slips like the dry cleaning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you used to be, say five years ago, have you noticed any real difference in your ability to remember things or stay on top of your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, five years ago? I mean, yeah, I guess there's been a change. Lately, I've been forgetting things I actually meant to do. Like, I was supposed to return a call from my doctor's office about some blood work, and it just completely slipped my mind. And picking up my prescription, too. I went all the way to the pharmacy, but then I realized I hadn't even checked if it was ready, and it wasn't. The pharmacist there, he's a nice guy, always has a bowl of those little strawberry candies on the counter. You know the ones with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? My grandmother used to keep those in her purse. She had this huge leather purse, weighed a ton. She used to carry around a roll of quarters in it, just in case she needed to make a phone call or pay for parking. Parking meters used to take quarters, not these fancy credit card apps they have now. You have to download an app just to park downtown! It’s ridiculous. I parked down by the courthouse last month and stood there for ten minutes trying to scan a barcode on the meter... \n\nAnyway, I also ended up leaving the house late the other day when I was supposed to take my cousin to one of his appointments. Just couldn't get out the door on time. So yeah, I guess I am a little more forgetful with my daily stuff than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: My wife? I mean, she hasn't said, \"Hey, you're more forgetful now.\" We've been married for thirty-two years, so she's pretty used to how I am. We actually met at a roller rink. Can you believe that? I kept hugging the wall, but she was out there doing crossovers and spinning. She wore these bright yellow leg warmers. Everybody wore leg warmers back then, even if they weren't going to the gym. The dust gets everywhere, up your nose, in your eyes, and those bales are heavy. Especially if they get a little wet before you get them in the barn. We had this huge red barn, classic style, with a tin roof that sounded amazing when it rained... \n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking about a year ago, right? I don't get confused by what people are saying, and I can follow a TV show just fine. Well, mostly. Yeah, if I'm actually listening, I understand it just fine. It's just that half the time my brain is off thinking about squirrels or something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or focus on tasks, like reading or following a conversation, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. It's really frustrating, actually. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order for the church, or I'm listening through one of those long parish planning calls... I start off fine. I'm right there, pen in hand. But before long, my mind just completely wanders off. I'll realize I haven't heard a word anyone said for the last five minutes, and I have to go back over the same notes like I never even read them. \n\nWhich reminds me, the catering for the church picnic last year was a disaster. We ordered from this new deli that opened up where the old bakery used to be. You know, the bakery that had those amazing bear claws? My wife and I used to go there every Sunday after service. The owner, Sal, he was such a character. Always had a joke ready. He retired to Florida, I think. Boca Raton, maybe? Or Naples. Somewhere warm. Anyway, this new deli, they completely messed up the potato salad. Put way too much mustard in it. People were complaining all afternoon. But yeah, trying to focus on those planning calls... it's like my brain just packs up and leaves the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble making decisions, solving problems, or figuring out the steps to complete tasks you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can still figure things out just fine, make decisions, all that. Like, if I need to fix something around the house or plan out my week, I know how to do it. It's just getting myself to actually finish it that's the problem. \n\nSpeaking of fixing things, I finally got around to looking at the lawnmower last weekend. It's one of those riding mowers, a John Deere. Green and yellow, you know the ones. I bought it second-hand from my neighbor, Gary, when he moved to a condo downtown. Gary's a good guy, but he never really took care of his tools. I had to replace the spark plugs and clean out the carburetor. It's amazing how much gunk builds up in there if you don't use the right fuel stabilizer over the winter. I always use the blue stuff, not the red stuff. The guy at the hardware store—Hank, I think his name is, wears these thick glasses—he swore by the blue one. Said the red one gums up the works on the newer models. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Making decisions. Yeah, I don't have any trouble knowing what to do or how to do it. I just... I get sidetracked before I actually get it done.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating questions or not remembering recent conversations, even after they've happened?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is actually pretty sharp. I don't forget what people tell me or ask the same thing over and over. I mean, just yesterday I was talking to my brother, Rick, and I can tell you exactly what we talked about. He just bought this pontoon boat for the lake up north. You know those big red plastic cans? They don't really make them like that anymore, now it's all weird cardboard or whatever. No, I remember conversations just fine. The desk is this beautiful old oak roll-top I found at an estate sale a few years back. The guy who owned it was an architect, I think. Had all these little cubby holes perfect for sorting mail. \n\nI had to refinish the whole thing myself, though. The original varnish was completely cracked and peeling. Took me three weekends just to strip it down, smelling like turpentine the whole time. My wife made me sleep in the guest room because she couldn't stand the smell. The guest room mattress is terrible, by the way. It's one of those old spring ones that pokes you right in the ribs. We really need to get a new one, maybe one of those memory foam things they sell in the boxes now. Have you ever tried one of those? Yeah, no, I can plan out the bills and follow recipes and all that without any real trouble. The recovery for that is brutal. He was practically living in our recliner in the living room for the first month. It’s a La-Z-Boy, one of the really plush microfiber ones. Chocolate brown. We got it on sale at this furniture outlet out by the highway. \n\nThe salesman there was a real character, kept trying to upsell us on the stain-resistant coating. I told him, look buddy, we don't have little kids running around spilling juice boxes anymore, we don't need the coating. But he just kept pushing it. Said it protects against pet hair too, which doesn't make any sense because we don't even have a dog. I always wanted a golden retriever, though. They're such loyal dogs. My neighbor growing up had one named Buster who used to fetch the morning paper... \n\nWait, what was I talking about? Oh, right. Yeah, my wife has definitely noticed it's been a lot harder for me to stay focused for the last 11 months or so.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to remember important information or handle daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: Compared to a year ago? Mine is just a little six-foot aluminum step ladder, totally useless for reaching the second story. \n\nMy neighbor, Tom, he has all the best tools. He used to be a general contractor before he retired. He's got this massive workshop in his garage with a table saw, a drill press, the works. No, no changes really. My memory and getting my daily chores done is pretty much exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks compared to how you were a year ago?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? But like I was saying, no, not really. I feel like I'm managing my daily stuff about the same as I was last year. My routine hasn't really changed at all. \n\nWell, except I did start doing the grocery shopping on Tuesday mornings instead of Thursdays. Tuesday is the senior discount day at the supermarket down the street. You get five percent off the whole cart. It doesn't sound like much, but it really adds up, especially with the price of groceries lately. Have you seen how much a carton of eggs costs now? It's absolute highway robbery. I remember when I was a kid, my grandfather kept his own chickens in the backyard so we never had to buy eggs. Rhode Island Reds, I think they were. Mean birds, though. One of them pecked me right on the shin when I was trying to help him feed them. I actually still have a tiny little white scar right there... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. No decline. My memory and how I handle things is exactly the same as it was a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any changes in your ability to learn new information, like figuring out how to use a new gadget or remembering names that you didn’t used to struggle with?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I don't think that's changed. I mean, gadgets are always a pain to figure out, but no more than usual. My son actually got me one of those smart thermostats for Christmas. You know, the round ones that light up when you walk by? It's supposed to save money on the heating bill by learning your schedule. But you have to set the whole thing up on your phone. I spent an hour just trying to get the Wi-Fi password typed in because the buttons on the screen are so tiny. My thumbs are just too big for those little digital keyboards. \n\nI really miss my old Blackberry. I had one back in the day with the actual physical buttons. That was a great phone. You could type a whole email without even looking down at the screen once you got used to it. I miss that tactile click, you know? Everything is just flat glass now. And you drop it once and it shatters into a million pieces. My nephew dropped his tablet on his driveway last month and the screen looked like a spiderweb. Cost him two hundred bucks to get the glass replaced at one of those little kiosks in the middle of the mall. The kid working there was practically a teenager himself, probably couldn't even buy a lottery ticket yet, fixing these expensive computers with a tiny screwdriver... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Gadgets, right. No, honestly, it takes me the exact same amount of time to figure that stuff out as it did last year. No real difference there.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you mentioned that you've had trouble remembering things or managing daily tasks in ways that you might not be noticing yourself?\n\nPatient: My wife? No, she hasn't said anything like that. I mean, she gets on my case about leaving my coffee mug on the coffee table instead of putting it in the dishwasher, but she's always done that. She likes things to be perfectly tidy. You should see her closet. Everything is color-coded. All the blues together, then the greens, then the reds. She even has her shoes lined up by heel height. It's impressive, honestly. \n\nI tried organizing my tool bench like that once. I bought one of those pegboards and all the little hooks. I spent a whole Saturday outlining where the hammers go, where the wrenches go... I even used a permanent marker to draw little silhouettes for each tool. It looked great for about a week. But yeah, like I said, no. Not anything important, anyway. \n\nI mean, she got a little annoyed a couple weeks ago because I forgot to pick up the dry cleaning on my way back from the hardware store. Her sister, Susan, she married this guy who owns a string of car washes out in the suburbs. You know the ones with the giant blue spinning brushes? I always worry those things are going to scratch the clear coat on my truck. I have a '98 Ford, still runs like an absolute dream. It only has about eighty thousand miles on it because I hardly drive it anymore. The secret is changing the oil every three thousand miles, religiously. People say you can wait until five or even ten thousand miles now with that new synthetic stuff, but I just don't trust it. My dad was a mechanic and he always said oil is the lifeblood of an engine, you let it get dirty and you're just asking for a blown gasket... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did I forget something? Oh, right, my wife pointing things out. No, she hasn't said I'm struggling or forgetting important stuff. Just normal everyday slips like the dry cleaning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you used to be, say five years ago, have you noticed any real difference in your ability to remember things or stay on top of your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, five years ago? I mean, yeah, I guess there's been a change. Lately, I've been forgetting things I actually meant to do. Like, I was supposed to return a call from my doctor's office about some blood work, and it just completely slipped my mind. And picking up my prescription, too. I went all the way to the pharmacy, but then I realized I hadn't even checked if it was ready, and it wasn't. The pharmacist there, he's a nice guy, always has a bowl of those little strawberry candies on the counter. You know the ones with the wrappers that look like actual strawberries? My grandmother used to keep those in her purse. She had this huge leather purse, weighed a ton. She used to carry around a roll of quarters in it, just in case she needed to make a phone call or pay for parking. Parking meters used to take quarters, not these fancy credit card apps they have now. You have to download an app just to park downtown! It’s ridiculous. I parked down by the courthouse last month and stood there for ten minutes trying to scan a barcode on the meter... \n\nAnyway, I also ended up leaving the house late the other day when I was supposed to take my cousin to one of his appointments. Just couldn't get out the door on time. So yeah, I guess I am a little more forgetful with my daily stuff than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife or anyone close to you noticed that your forgetfulness or difficulty focusing has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: My wife? Well, we just talked about her, didn't we? But no, she hasn't said anything about it getting worse over the last year. I mean, she hasn't said, \"Hey, you're more forgetful now.\" We've been married for thirty-two years, so she's pretty used to how I am. We actually met at a roller rink. Can you believe that? They used to have those couples' skates where they'd play a slow song, usually something by Chicago or maybe REO Speedwagon. I wasn't a very good skater, honestly. I kept hugging the wall, but she was out there doing crossovers and spinning. She wore these bright yellow leg warmers. Everybody wore leg warmers back then, even if they weren't going to the gym. It was just the style. Like those shoulder pads in the blazers? My sister had a blazer with shoulder pads so big she looked like a linebacker for the Bears. She loved the Bears. We all did. The '85 season, man, that was something else. Walter Payton... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Has she noticed it getting worse? No, I don't think she's noticed any big change in the last year or so. Just the usual stuff we talked about.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed any real change in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks effectively?\n\nPatient: Well, a year ago? Let me think... I guess it has been noticeably harder for about, oh, eleven months now. Yeah, right around then. That's when my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. He had his knee replaced. The right one, I think. Or maybe the left? No, it was the right, because he couldn't drive his truck for a while, and it's an automatic. If it was a manual, he really would have been out of luck with the clutch. You don't see many manual transmissions anymore, do you? I learned to drive on a three-on-the-tree. My uncle had this old Chevy pickup, farm truck, really. We used to haul hay in the back of it during the summer. Baling hay is tough work, let me tell you. The dust gets everywhere, up your nose, in your eyes, and those bales are heavy. Especially if they get a little wet before you get them in the barn. We had this huge red barn, classic style, with a tin roof that sounded amazing when it rained... \n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking about a year ago, right? Yeah, so like I said, about eleven months ago, ever since my cousin came to stay with us, things have definitely been harder to manage. Just a lot more to keep track of, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were a year ago, have you noticed a definite decline in your ability to remember things or manage your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really a definite decline, I don't think. I mean, I just mentioned the stuff with my cousin moving in, but it's not like my brain is suddenly working worse than it was exactly twelve months ago. It's just... more chaotic, you know? But a definite decline? No, I wouldn't say that. \n\nSpeaking of decline, though, you should see the maple tree in our front yard. It’s been looking really sad lately. Half the leaves turned brown before September even started. My neighbor, Gary, he thinks it’s some kind of fungus. Gary used to work for the parks department, so he knows a lot about trees. He’s got this incredible garden in his backyard—tomatoes as big as softballs. He uses this special compost he makes himself. He tried to explain the ratio of green matter to brown matter to me once, but I lost track. He saves all his eggshells and coffee grounds. I tried saving coffee grounds once, but they just got moldy under the sink. My wife was not happy about that smell. It was awful, like old gym socks mixed with burnt toast. I had to throw the whole container out. It was one of those nice Tupperware ones with the blue lids, too... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I haven't noticed any major drop-off or decline in my memory compared to last year. Just the same old stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
